### Stop Summation: Min-Form Credit Assignment Is All Process Reward Model Needs for Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.15275v1)
- **Authors**: Jie Cheng, Ruixi Qiao, Lijun Li, Chao Guo, Junle Wang, Gang Xiong, Yisheng Lv, Fei-Yue Wang
- **Abstract**: Process reward models (PRMs) have proven effective for test-time scaling of Large Language Models (LLMs) on challenging reasoning tasks. However, reward hacking issues with PRMs limit their successful application in reinforcement fine-tuning. In this paper, we identify the main cause of PRM-induced reward hacking: the canonical summation-form credit assignment in reinforcement learning (RL), which defines the value as cumulative gamma-decayed future rewards, easily induces LLMs to hack steps with high rewards. To address this, we propose PURE: Process sUpervised Reinforcement lEarning. The key innovation of PURE is a min-form credit assignment that formulates the value function as the minimum of future rewards. This method significantly alleviates reward hacking by limiting the value function range and distributing advantages more reasonably. Through extensive experiments on 3 base models, we show that PRM-based approaches enabling min-form credit assignment achieve comparable reasoning performance to verifiable reward-based methods within only 30% steps. In contrast, the canonical sum-form credit assignment collapses training even at the beginning! Additionally, when we supplement PRM-based fine-tuning with just 10% verifiable rewards, we further alleviate reward hacking and produce the best fine-tuned model based on Qwen2.5-Math-7B in our experiments, achieving 82.5% accuracy on AMC23 and 53.3% average accuracy across 5 benchmarks. Moreover, we summarize the observed reward hacking cases and analyze the causes of training collapse. Code and models are available at https://github.com/CJReinforce/PURE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Stop Summation: Min-Form Credit Assignment Is All Process Reward Model Needs for Reasoning" addresses the issue of reward hacking in reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) of large language models (LLMs) when using process reward models (PRMs).  The authors identify the summation-form credit assignment commonly used in RL as a key contributor to this problem, as it encourages LLMs to optimize for individual steps with high rewards rather than overall problem-solving.  They propose a new approach called PURE (Process sUpervised Reinforcement lEarning), which uses a min-form credit assignment.  This method defines the value function as the minimum of future rewards, aiming to limit the value function range and distribute advantages more reasonably. Through experiments with three base models, the authors demonstrate that PURE achieves comparable reasoning performance to verifiable reward-based methods, and that the addition of few ground-truth signals further mitigates reward hacking. The paper also analyzes observed reward hacking cases and the causes of training collapse.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty of the paper lies in the *min-form credit assignment* within the context of PRM-based RFT for LLMs. While PRMs have been explored before, the specific problem of reward hacking in their application to LLM reasoning tasks hasn't been thoroughly investigated. The connection the authors make between summation-based credit assignment and this reward hacking, and the alternative min-form proposal, is a valuable contribution.  The paper offers a distinct perspective that separates it from other research that mainly focuses on verifiable reward strategies.
* **Significance:**  The significance comes from the potential to make PRM-based RFT more effective and stable. PRMs offer the advantage of dense feedback, which is crucial for long reasoning chains where sparse rewards can be insufficient.  If PURE successfully mitigates reward hacking, it could unlock the potential of PRMs to improve LLMs' reasoning abilities more efficiently than purely verifiable reward approaches. The reported gains in efficiency (achieving comparable performance with 30% of the training steps) are a compelling indicator of this potential. The analysis of different types of reward hacking and the training collapse phenomena adds practical value for researchers and practitioners in the field.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of reward hacking and pinpoints the summation-form credit assignment as a major cause.
    *   **Well-Motivated Solution:** The min-form credit assignment is logically motivated by the test-time use of PRMs.
    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Evaluation:**  The authors evaluate their method on multiple base models, reward configurations, and benchmark datasets, providing strong empirical evidence for the effectiveness of PURE.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:**  The paper includes a valuable analysis of reward hacking cases and the causes of training collapse, offering insights into the challenges of PRM-based RFT.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The analysis of the training collapses reveals complex interactions between various factors. In particular, the role of generative PRMs is acknowledged, but not extensively investigated in this work. Exploring these connections and attempting to incorporate such improvements is a reasonable step.
    * The paper could benefit from a comparison against other methods that aim to alleviate reward hacking in general RL, even if they aren't specifically tailored to LLMs and PRMs. It would strengthen the claims of novelty.
    * Although the efficiency of min-form credit assignment is highlighted, more data on the trade-offs associated with verifiable rewards would be beneficial.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to significantly influence the direction of research in RFT of LLMs for reasoning tasks. It could encourage further investigation into alternative credit assignment methods and the development of more robust training strategies for PRM-based RFT. The detailed analysis of reward hacking could also serve as a valuable resource for researchers working in this area.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper makes a notable contribution by addressing a specific problem in a relevant field (LLM reasoning) with a distinct and experimentally substantiated approach. The min-form credit assignment is a valuable addition to the current methods. The provided analysis will serve the community in further improving similar methods. Nevertheless, there are some weaknesses, particularly with regard to the analysis of the relationship between generative PRMs and some trade-offs with existing methods. The limitations keep the score from the top tier.

### VisuLogic: A Benchmark for Evaluating Visual Reasoning in Multi-modal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.15279v1)
- **Authors**: Weiye Xu, Jiahao Wang, Weiyun Wang, Zhe Chen, Wengang Zhou, Aijun Yang, Lewei Lu, Houqiang Li, Xiaohua Wang, Xizhou Zhu, Wenhai Wang, Jifeng Dai, Jinguo Zhu
- **Abstract**: Visual reasoning is a core component of human intelligence and a critical capability for advanced multimodal models. Yet current reasoning evaluations of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often rely on text descriptions and allow language-based reasoning shortcuts, failing to measure genuine vision-centric reasoning. To address this, we introduce VisuLogic: a benchmark of 1,000 human-verified problems across six categories (e.g., quantitative shifts, spatial relations, attribute comparisons). These various types of questions can be evaluated to assess the visual reasoning capabilities of MLLMs from multiple perspectives. We evaluate leading MLLMs on this benchmark and analyze their results to identify common failure modes. Most models score below 30% accuracy-only slightly above the 25% random baseline and far below the 51.4% achieved by humans-revealing significant gaps in visual reasoning. Furthermore, we provide a supplementary training dataset and a reinforcement-learning baseline to support further progress.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VisuLogic: A Benchmark for Evaluating Visual Reasoning in Multi-modal Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VisuLogic, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the visual reasoning capabilities of Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The benchmark comprises 1,000 human-verified problems across six categories designed to stress genuine vision-centric reasoning, minimizing language-based shortcuts. The authors evaluate several leading MLLMs on the benchmark, analyze their results, and identify common failure modes.  They also provide a supplementary training dataset and a reinforcement-learning baseline to encourage further progress in the field. The results reveal that current MLLMs perform poorly on the benchmark, significantly underperforming humans, highlighting a considerable gap in visual reasoning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the explicit design of a benchmark that minimizes language-based reasoning shortcuts and focuses on visual cognitive processes. While other benchmarks exist for multimodal reasoning, VisuLogic seems more deliberately geared towards *pure* visual reasoning, which is a plus. The idea to create a dataset specifically crafted to require true visual reasoning and avoid textual shortcuts is interesting.

*   **Significance:** The benchmark's significance lies in its potential to drive the development of more robust and genuinely visually capable MLLMs. The disappointing performance of current models on VisuLogic highlights the limitations of relying on textual descriptions or exploiting language-based shortcuts. The release of a supplementary training dataset and RL baseline has a potential to foster future research and innovation in this area. By specifically targeting the visual component, the benchmark could significantly impact how MLLMs are developed and evaluated.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear articulation of the problem and the motivation for a new benchmark.
    *   Well-defined categories of visual reasoning, covering a range of cognitive skills.
    *   Rigorous human verification of problems.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of multiple MLLMs.
    *   Provision of a supplementary training dataset and baseline.
    *   The paper highlights an important shortcoming in the current generation of MLLMs and provides resources (benchmark, data, baseline) for future development.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the data generation process and rationale behind the specific types of visual reasoning tasks chosen. What biases (if any) are inherent in their task selection? How do they deal with potential cultural biases that might influence human answers?
    *   The paper mentions using RL fine-tuning. It might be helpful to provide a stronger theoretical justification or prior works supporting why using RL will make improvements to the model. A stronger explanation is needed of the RL fine-tuning process, as well as experiments testing the effect of using RL on different architecture and models (including open-source and closed-source architectures).
    *   The dataset is small (1000 examples). Although well-curated, the limited size could restrict the generalizability of findings. Also, the supplementary training set is about ~4x the test set. It might be worthwhile to explore larger ratios in order to improve the performance for the MLLMs and further explore this method.
    *   While the human accuracy is given, the paper doesn't discuss the distribution of human responses (e.g., inter-annotator agreement). This might provide valuable information about the difficulty of the task.
    *   The benchmark might not be truly free of language bias. While minimized, subtle linguistic cues in the questions might still influence the models' responses.

*   **Potential Influence:**  If widely adopted, VisuLogic could become a standard benchmark for visual reasoning in MLLMs. This could shift the focus of research from simply improving overall performance to developing more visually competent models. The benchmark's limitations (size, specific task design) also highlight potential avenues for future research, prompting the creation of larger, more diverse, and more carefully controlled visual reasoning datasets.

*   **Score Justification:**
    I'm assigning a score of 7/10. While the paper addresses a significant need and presents a novel benchmark, the relatively small dataset size, and missing justification for the RL method does limit its overall impact. Nevertheless, the creation of VisuLogic and the resources made available to the research community have the potential to advance visual reasoning research significantly. The key contribution lies in its targeted approach to minimizing textual shortcuts and forcing models to engage in genuine vision-centric reasoning.

### Seeing from Another Perspective: Evaluating Multi-View Understanding in MLLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.15280v1)
- **Authors**: Chun-Hsiao Yeh, Chenyu Wang, Shengbang Tong, Ta-Ying Cheng, Rouyu Wang, Tianzhe Chu, Yuexiang Zhai, Yubei Chen, Shenghua Gao, Yi Ma
- **Abstract**: Multi-view understanding, the ability to reconcile visual information across diverse viewpoints for effective navigation, manipulation, and 3D scene comprehension, is a fundamental challenge in Multi-Modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to be used as embodied agents. While recent MLLMs have shown impressive advances in high-level reasoning and planning, they frequently fall short when confronted with multi-view geometric consistency and cross-view correspondence. To comprehensively evaluate the challenges of MLLMs in multi-view scene reasoning, we propose All-Angles Bench, a benchmark of over 2,100 human carefully annotated multi-view question-answer pairs across 90 diverse real-world scenes. Our six tasks (counting, attribute identification, relative distance, relative direction, object manipulation, and camera pose estimation) specifically test model's geometric correspondence and the capacity to align information consistently across views. Our extensive experiments, benchmark on 27 representative MLLMs including Gemini-2.0-Flash, Claude-3.7-Sonnet, and GPT-4o against human evaluators reveals a substantial performance gap, indicating that current MLLMs remain far from human-level proficiency. Through in-depth analysis, we show that MLLMs are particularly underperforming under two aspects: (1) cross-view correspondence for partially occluded views and (2) establishing the coarse camera poses. These findings highlight the necessity of domain-specific refinements or modules that embed stronger multi-view awareness. We believe that our All-Angles Bench offers valuable insights and contribute to bridging the gap between MLLMs and human-level multi-view understanding. The project and benchmark are publicly available at https://danielchyeh.github.io/All-Angles-Bench/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces All-Angles Bench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate multi-view understanding capabilities in Multi-Modal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The benchmark consists of over 2,100 carefully annotated question-answer pairs based on 90 diverse real-world scenes captured from multiple viewpoints. The paper defines six tasks: counting, attribute identification, relative distance, relative direction, object manipulation, and camera pose estimation. These tasks specifically test the geometric consistency and cross-view correspondence abilities of MLLMs. The paper benchmarks 27 representative MLLMs, including prominent models like Gemini-2.0-Flash, Claude-3.7-Sonnet, and GPT-4o, comparing their performance to human evaluators. The results reveal a significant performance gap between current MLLMs and human-level multi-view understanding. The paper also presents an in-depth analysis of MLLMs' weaknesses, particularly in cross-view correspondence for partially occluded views and in establishing coarse camera poses. Finally, the paper explores the effectiveness of chain-of-thought prompting strategies and finds limited improvement for multi-view reasoning, suggesting the need for domain-specific refinements or modules that embed stronger multi-view awareness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a relatively underexplored area in MLLM evaluation - multi-view understanding. While existing benchmarks focus primarily on single-view or egocentric data, All-Angles Bench directly tackles the challenge of reconciling visual information from diverse viewpoints. This is a clear strength and addresses a crucial gap in the evaluation landscape. The creation of paired questions is also a good attempt to further assess the robustness of multi-view understanding, going beyond single question accuracy.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significant implications for the development of embodied agents and systems that require robust 3D scene understanding. The benchmark reveals critical shortcomings in current MLLMs, highlighting areas that need improvement for real-world applications like navigation, manipulation, and interaction. The analysis of failure cases and identification of key challenges (e.g., cross-view correspondence with partial occlusion and camera pose estimation) provides valuable insights for future research directions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The benchmark is well-defined and comprehensive, covering a variety of tasks and real-world scenes.
    *   The careful annotation process and quality control measures ensure the reliability of the benchmark.
    *   The in-depth analysis of MLLM performance and identification of specific weaknesses provides valuable insights.
    *   The exploration of chain-of-thought prompting provides a preliminary investigation into potential mitigation strategies.
    *   The work is well written and clearly presents the motivation, methodology, and results.
    *   The public availability of the benchmark promotes further research and development in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from exploring more sophisticated architectures or training techniques explicitly designed to improve multi-view understanding beyond just prompting strategies.
    *   The evaluation focuses primarily on accuracy.  Exploring more fine-grained metrics related to confidence, uncertainty, and the consistency of internal representations across views could further enrich the analysis.
    * The dataset size, while significant, is still limited compared to benchmarks used for single-view tasks. Scaling up the dataset could potentially reveal further weaknesses in MLLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The All-Angles Bench has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating multi-view understanding in MLLMs. It can drive research towards developing more robust and capable models for embodied AI and other applications that require 3D scene understanding. The insights provided in the paper can guide future research directions in areas such as multi-view fusion, geometric reasoning, and spatial awareness.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** While the paper's novelty and significance are apparent, the exploration of solutions to the identified challenges remains limited. The evaluation focuses primarily on accuracy, without deeper probing of the reasoning processes of the models. A stronger dive into potential architectural or training-data related improvements could have made the paper stronger.
